3.3 Genetic algorithm

GAs are fundamentally inspired by Charles Darwin’s natural selection process, where they accept input and generate output by selecting from various potential solutions. GAs were initially employed by John Holland [15]. The GAs are intended to mimic natural system processes that are required for evolution. To find the best answers, GA searches efficiently in global spaces. GA is more successful at enhancing contrast and generates natural-looking photos. A GA is a search technique inspired by the principles of natural selection mechanism and genetic system. These algorithms are highly parallel and flexible, merging the concept of “survival of the fittest” in string structures with a systematic yet random exchange of information. This approach results in a search strategy that mimics some of the creativity found in human problem-solving. They effectively use previous data to predict and explore new search areas with projected improved outcomes, iteratively applying genetically inspired operators to probable solutions.

Algorithm 1: Pseudo of the Genetic Algorithm

g = 0 /*Initialize Generation counter*/ P = Initialize popultaion/*Random generation of p individuals */ Repeat UNTIL end condition
F = Reproducstion(P)/*Selection of p parents thriugh a roulette*/
 S = Crossover(F)
M = Mutation (S)/*Alteration of the individual bits with probability mc*/
P = Updtae population(M)/*M is copied to P(P=M)*/
 g = g + 1/*Increment of the Generation counter */±

Unlike traditional search strategies, GAs deal with numerous points at once rather than a single point. GAs have been shown to give reliable search in complex spaces both theoretically and experimentally. Pattern recognition and image processing present numerous challenges that require executing efficient searches within complex spaces to identify the optimal solution. GAs use three fundamental procedures on a restricted number of strings to discover the global near-optimal answer. Reproduction/selection, cross-over, and mutation are the three operators.

3.3.1 Parent selection strategies

3.3.1.1 Tournament selection

One of the selection strategies used in GAs is tournament Selection. In this, the top candidates are selected from the present generation. After this, the selected best candidates are advanced to the following generation. In a K-way tournament selection, top k-individuals are selected and they compete against each other [16]. Among the selected candidates only the best candidate is selected and passed on to the next generation. Numerous tournaments are conducted, allowing us to finalize the pick of candidates, those will advance to the following generation.

This selection strategy has a feature called selection pressure, that provides a probabilistic evaluation of a candidate’s chance of being chosen for a tournament. If the tournament size is large, weaker candidates will have less likelihood of selection, as they must compete against stronger opponents. The pace of convergence of the GA is determined by the selection pressure parameter. The higher the selection pressure, the higher the rate of convergence. Over a wide range of selection pressures, GAs can uncover best or near-best solutions.

3.3.1.2 Roulette wheel selection

In roulette wheel selection, the circular wheel is divided into segments representing individuals. Where each segment size is proportional to the fitness of the individual, as represented in Figure 3.2. The wheel is rotated and a specific point on the perimeter of the wheel is selected as indicated in the figure. The segments that comes in front of that specific point is chosen as a parent [17]. Similar process is followed for choosing the next parent. When the wheel is revolved, an individual with better fitness will have more probability of stopping in front of the designated point, as it has the larger segment on the wheel. Hence, likelihood of selecting an individual is directly related to his fitness value.

3.3.1.3 Ranking selection

In rank selection, the population is first ranked, and then each chromosome’s fitness is assigned based on its position in that ranking. The worst chromosome receives a fitness value of 1, the second-worst gets a fitness of 2, and so forth, with the best chromosome receiving a fitness value equal to N, where N is the total number of chromosomes in the population [18]. Following that, all chromosomes have a chance to be chosen. Premature convergence can be avoided using rank-based selection techniques. However, because it sorts the populations based on fitness value, it can be computationally expensive. However, because the best chromosomes do not differ as much from the others, this strategy may result in slower convergence.

3.3.2 Crossover

Crossover is a genetic characteristic that involves the joining of two chromosomes (also known as parents) to form a new chromosome (also called offspring). If the new chromosome inherits the best qualities from both parents, the result of crossover may be better than both parents [19] as shown in Figure 3.3. During evolution, crossover happens according to a user-defined crossover probability. The new offspring will inherit certain characteristics from one parent and others from the other. Assume first parent is 10001011 and second parent is 10011111; after completing the crossover, the outcome can be 10011111, which has some part of the first parent and some part of the second parent.

3.3.3 Mutation

After the crossover is completed, mutation may occur. Which is done to avoid all population solutions falling into a local best of the current problem. The encoding as well as the crossing influences the mutation. When encoding permutations, for example, the mutation could mean switching two genes [20]. The new offspring are randomly altered by mutation as shown in Figure 3.4. We can encode binary data by altering a few randomly selected bits from 1 to 0 or 0 to 1. The mutations for the parent 10001011 could be 10001111 and for the parent 10011111 could be 10010111.

3.3.4 Formulation of objective function and fitness function

The objective function is used to determine a solution’s worth. The output of this objective function is then turned into a fitness value that is utilized to determine the quality of a solution. Because GA is an optimization tool, the objective function is often a maximization or minimization function that includes all variables. The fitness function was designed to create a bimodal histogram in the grey level intensities, allowing for automated image processing steps to evaluate the quality of the individuals throughout the optimization. Individuals with a histogram with two well-separated normal distributions and an equal distance from the optimal threshold are favored by the fitness function we utilize.

GAs have numerous benefits over classical optimization algorithms. These algorithms are very versatile and are capable of handling a wide range of objective functions. The objective function may be stable or dynamic, linear or nonlinear, continuous or discontinuous, or even affected by random noise. The ability of GAs to tackle complex problems and their inherent their inherent parallelism are the two most notable advantages. Because multiple offspring in a population operate independently, the population (or any subset) can hunt in multiple directions at the same time. This capability of GAs makes the parallelization of algorithms very easy, and it also simplifies the modification of different parameters and even groups of encoded strings. However, GAs have some drawbacks as well. The selection criteria for the new population, the design of objective function, the choice of population size, and the selection of key factors like mutation and crossover rates must be handled with utmost care. Any misstep in the choices of these parameters can make it difficult for the algorithm to converge optimally and resulting in ineffective outcomes. Despite these limitations, GAs are nevertheless extensively used nonlinear optimization strategies today.

As a result, GAs is being used extensively in the field of engineering over the past decade. GAs are now being seen as the powerful tool for design optimization. This research work explores the different applications of evolutionary algorithms to solve complex optimization problems, challenges, and managing uncertainty in typical industrial scenarios. Here, the GA is utilized as an optimization mechanism in the supply chain planning process under uncertainty to determine the best production, inventory level, and distribution strategies.

3.4 Methodology

This research work aims to create a framework for creating the finest possible images by using an algorithm that considers all restrictions to produce better outcomes. For accurate and efficient picture enhancement, it employs the GA. It takes a picture as input and then enhances it. We start by interpreting an image and setting up the fitness feature. The GA begins with a randomly produced image that is similar in shape to the input image. This randomly generated image is continuously evolved utilizing crossover and mutation, as well as GA, until it achieves a better solution than the original.

3.4.1 Data representation

The initial step in solving a GA optimization problem is to consider the best way to describe the data. The chromosome (i.e. solution) is accepted as a 1D row vector by GA, but the input image is not one-dimensional. If the image is binary or grayscale, it may be 2D. If the input image is color, there may be more than two dimensions. If the color is RGB, there are three dimensions, one for each channel. A 1D vector must be used to represent data with more than one dimension. How can we turn multidimensional (MD) data into a 1D vector?

Starting with the simplest scenario, transforming a 2D image (or 2D matrix) into a 1D vector necessitates merging the two dimensions into a single dimension as shown in Figure 3.5. The matrix has numerous rows, and all of these rows must be merged into a single row. This is accomplished by stacking the several rows together. The following diagram depicts this. The illustration depicts a three-row, three-column image/matrix. There are 3 × 3 = 9 elements in all.

The vector length will undoubtedly be 9 when converted to a 1D vector. The first three elements of this vector will be drawn from the image’s first row. The vector’s next three items will come from the second row, and the vector’s last three elements will come from the last row.

3.4.2 Fitness function

The fitness or objective function is a key segment of the GA, as it determines the quality of each image iteration based on a set of quantitative indices. As greyness and spatial ambiguity measurements for image enhancement and segmentation challenges, the proposed technique uses entropy (H), compactness (COMP), index of area coverage (IOAC), and their combinations as quantitative indices for evaluating picture quality. The average amount of fuzziness in the greyness of an image (X) is the degree of difficulty (ambiguity) in identifying whether a pixel should be considered black (dark) or white (bright) based on global information. Compactness and IOAC take local information into consideration and indicate the degree of fuzziness in an image’s shape and geometry (spatial domain). As a result, the concept of minimizing these ambiguous measurements can be used to build a fitness (assessment) function.

During each iteration, the fitness function calculates a composite score by applying a weighted formula that integrates these indices. Higher scores indicate better-defined image features and clearer contrasts, guiding the GA to select and breed images that move closer to an optimal, high-quality output. By evolving images towards a bimodal histogram with distinct peaks in intensity, the fitness function drives the enhancement process, ensuring the GA consistently favors improved clarity and contrast.

3.5 Experimentation and results

GA is utilized in a range of complicated problems; a second level job must be used to regulate their parameters for efficiency’s sake. As previously stated, the rate of mutation and crossover is known to have a significant impact on the efficiency and behavior of GA. Some researchers utilize tuning of the different parameters to select them, such that it identifies the best value of the parameters before the execution and then run the GA with these. Alternatively, other researchers have proved that utilizing control parameters (deterministic, adaptive) is more efficient in generating best or near-best results.

According to certain studies, high crossover rates of 80–95% and modest mutation rates of (0.1–1) should be used. The researcher advises utilizing a high mutation e.g. 50% with crossover and a small population in another study. Without any feedback from the search process or user involvement, the deterministic control parameter alters the parameter value during the execution. As a result, this kind of feature setting should be utilized to try to figure out which behavior the parameter should deal with in order to be effective and to discover the best or near-best answer. Using high crossover rates with low mutation rates, along with a large population size, promotes diversity within the population. However, in smaller populations, higher mutation rates are employed to introduce diversity and enhance search quality. By adjusting crossover and mutation rates, GA efficiency can be improved through a balanced approach to population diversity.

3.5.1 Qualitative and quantitative results

Figures 3.6 and 3.7 show the input image and the enhanced image generated after applying the enhancement process using different numbers of generations and different crossover rate. The similar approach can also work on images other than those are related to medical field, as shown in Figures 3.8 and 3.9. As shown in these figures, increasing the number of generations and adjusting crossover rates significantly impacted the quality of the enhanced images. With an optimal combination of 500 generations, a 0.4 crossover rate, and a 1% mutation rate, the images achieved the highest clarity and contrast enhancement. These optimized parameters resulted in images where critical medical features were highlighted, allowing for easier anomaly detection in clinical settings.

3.5.2 Time efficiency and scalability

To assess scalability, tests were conducted on image datasets of various resolutions. While the GA performed efficiently for smaller image sets, higher-resolution images required significantly more processing time, underscoring a limitation of the framework in terms of computational cost. Further optimization or parallel processing could help address this limitation, making the GA framework more viable for large-scale applications.

3.6 Limitations

One of the main limitations of the proposed GA framework is the high-computational cost associated with iteratively evolving large datasets. Each generation requires a fitness evaluation, crossover, and mutation processes that can be computationally intensive, especially for high-resolution medical images. As the dataset size increases, so does the number of generations and operations required to converge on an optimal solution. This prolonged processing time may limit real-time applications, making the framework less suitable for scenarios where immediate image enhancement is essential.

Moreover, the GA’s reliance on numerous parameters—such as mutation rate, crossover rate, and population size—demands careful tuning to maintain efficiency, which can add further to the processing time if multiple configurations need to be tested. Future work could explore optimized parallel processing techniques or hybrid models that combine GAs with other, less resource-intensive algorithms to improve efficiency without compromising image quality.

3.7 Conclusion and future work

This work has proven to be an extremely important step for our project. This research has the potential to serve as a prototype for a wide variety of medical specialties across many different dimensions. It is possible to improve upon and retrieve with higher quality various ancient and old photographs that are typically blurry and a little bit on the lower side in terms of what is considered to be their overall quality. These images can then be stored for the future. The ability to learn about and work on the process of image enhancement utilizing cutting-edge techniques like GAs was one of the many things that I picked up from working on this project. The project can be developed further and put into use in the future by adding a few more features and attributes to it. These additions will make the project more user-friendly by making it more convenient and less difficult to use. In future, the integration of all the tools into a single web app or desktop program so that work can be done without interruption. Make an effort to develop additional application tools so that the process of improvement can be completed more quickly. Improve the output by enhancing the performance of the currently used method.

Chapter 4Machine learning security on drones or UAV

Meenal Jain and Kirti Aggarwal

DOI: 10.1201/9781003569619-4

4.1 Introduction

4.1.1 Working of drones

4.1.1.1 Network infrastructure of drones

To enable successful communication, control and data transmission, unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) or drones rely on a range of network infrastructure components. Understanding these infrastructural components is critical for understanding the intricacies of drone operations:

  1. Communication Links:
    • Radio Frequency (RF) Links: Drones often use RF links, such as 2.4 GHz and 5.8 GHz, are common for control and telemetry.
    • Wi-Fi: Consumer drones frequently utilise Wi-Fi connections for communication between the drone and a remote controller or smartphone app.
    • Cellular Networks: Commercial and enterprise-level drones are equipped with cellular connectivity, allowing for extended-range communication, data transfer and live video streaming.
    • Satellite Communication: In remote or offshore areas, drones may rely on satellite communication to maintain connectivity, ensuring global coverage.
  2. Data Links: Drones provide a variety of data kinds, such as photos, movies, sensor readings and telemetry data. Data connections, both cable and wireless, are used to transport data between the drone and ground systems.
  3. GPS Receivers: Most drones include GPS receivers, which allow them to detect their specific location and assure correct navigation. GPS data is an essential component of drone network architecture, allowing for safe and efficient flying.
  4. Telemetry Systems: Telemetry systems send real-time data about the drone’s status to the ground control station, including altitude, speed, battery level and sensor readings. This information is critical for tracking and managing the drone’s performance.
  5. Remote Controllers: Operators utilise remote controllers or transmitters to convey control commands to the drone. These controllers communicate with the drone via RF or Wi-Fi connectivity.

These numerous components compose the network backbone of drones, allowing them to perform efficiently in a variety of applications. The infrastructural elements chosen are determined by considerations such as the drone’s function, range requirements, operational environment and regulatory restraints. Understanding this infrastructure is critical for understanding the security issues and potential vulnerabilities that may be addressed in the context of drone operations.

Drones are susceptible to a variety of hostile assaults, such as data eavesdropping, GPS spoofing, radio frequency jamming and unauthorised access. This emphasises the urgent need for extensive security measures to safeguard both the drones and the private information they carry. Drone network architecture often incorporates security features including encryption, authentication, intrusion detection systems and network monitoring to prevent unauthorised access and data eavesdropping.

4.1.2 Adversarial attacks

A wide variety of algorithms have developed in the complex field of machine learning (ML) security, each one specifically designed to look for and take advantage of flaws in models. These algorithms primarily focus on the exciting field of adversarial attacks, which explores and manipulates the very underpinnings of ML models.

  1. L-BFGS Algorithm: The L-BFGS method is an aggressive optimisation approach. It is a white-box attack approach. L-BFGS generates adversarial samples by repeatedly improving perturbations on input data in order to optimise the model’s loss. This approach is successful, although it is computationally demanding.
  2. Fast Gradient Sign Method (FGSM): FGSM is a white-box attack algorithm that is both quick and efficient. It modifies the input data by performing a single step in the direction of the loss function’s gradient with respect to the input. Although FGSM produces adversarial cases fast, it does not always achieve high success rates.
  3. Basic Iterative Method (BIM) and Projected Gradient Descent (PGD): BIM and PGD are white-box iterative attack strategies. They create adversarial samples by doing numerous iterations of perturbation on input data. BIM begins with a modest perturbation and adds noise repeatedly to maximise the model’s loss. PGD is a more powerful form of BIM that projects perturbations onto a ball to keep them within a restricted range.
  4. Momentum Iterative Attack: The motion iterative method is a white-box iterative method that improves BIM by adding motion. It uses gradient information accumulated over iterations to make greater modifications to perturbations. This method aids in avoiding local optima and frequently yields stronger adversarial samples.
  5. GPS Spoofing: GPS spoofing is the sending of fake or altered GPS signals in order to fool GPS receivers (see Figure 4.1). The purpose of GPS spoofing is to trick the target device or system into thinking it is somewhere else than it is. GPS spoofing is especially troublesome in the case of drones or UAVs. Drones frequently rely on GPS for precise navigation, waypoint tracking and flying stability. When a drone is subjected to GPS spoofing, it can be misled into taking erroneous flight paths, possibly leading to dangerous scenarios. It can also jeopardise the security of operations that rely on precise location data.
  1. Distributionally Adversarial Attack: Individual samples are not targeted by distributionally adversarial assaults, which focus on perturbing data distributions. They strive to reduce the statistical divergence between the original and altered data distributions. These attacks evaluate model resilience by taking into account the complete data distribution and frequently demonstrate model transferability.
  2. Blockchain-based attack: A blockchain-based attack on a drone could take a number of different forms. For example, an attacker modify the drone’s data and disrupt the drone’s communication with its ground control station.

In conclusion, adversarial attack strategies are an essential and dynamic part of the ongoing endeavour to enhance the security of ML models. By actively probing and pushing these models with hostile inputs, security practitioners and researchers may uncover weaknesses, evaluate threats realistically and promote continuous development in the robustness and resilience of AI systems. Adversarial attack techniques are becoming more and more vital in preserving the reliability and integrity of AI-powered systems as ML expands into more critical industries.

4.2 Literature survey

Following a close review of the aforementioned research publications discussed in Table 4.1, it is clear that the multiple applications of UAVs in the military, public and technological sectors have resulted in significant advancement in a variety of disciplines. Advances in cybersecurity frameworks, safe autonomous navigation and trajectory planning-focused navigational systems, and secure communication for the Internet of Drones (IoD) are among them. The incorporation of satellite, 5G and UAV technologies into telecommunication networks has highlighted the importance of cybersecurity measures to avoid cyberattacks. ML techniques are utilised to improve security in UAV networks, specifically to distinguish between hostile and benign network traffic. An emphasis has also been placed on overcoming adversarial attack vulnerabilities in deep learning (DL) algorithms, as well as assessing the advantages and risks connected with the growing use of drones in armed services, healthcare and agricultural applications.

Table 4.1 Key contributions in UAV security and applications⏎
Author Summary
Samanth et al. [1] UAVs are used in both armed-services and public contexts. While armed-services applications include border surveillance and intruder reporting to Ground Control Stations (GCS), public usage include construction site and catastrophe monitoring. Proposed UAV frameworks are evaluated using open-source UAV simulators such as AirSim. The Internet of Drones (IoD) requires safe communication; studies of secure IoD communication frameworks use notions from cryptography. NS3 and OMNeT++ are popular network simulators used for this kind of study
Vattapparamban et al. [2] Drones are predicted to play a significant part in charge of maintaining smart city security and monitoring, transporting commodities and merchandise and acting as mobile hotspots for broadband wireless access. Drone usage for future smart cities will, however, also inevitably raise a number of technological and social issues that require attention. This survey article aims to analyse several drone-related topics related to cybersecurity and privacy. We will also share representative findings about drone-based cyberattacks
Shrestha et al. [3] Recent increases in the use of UAVs in everything from parcel delivery to wildlife protection are driving the advancement of telecommunication networks to incorporate 5G, satellite and UAV technology. But because UAV nodes are more and more vulnerable to hacks, security worries are developing. This study presents a machine learning-based 5G network security model that utilises UAVs and satellites to identify seven modern attack types. In order to improve security, the article shows how well ML systems can differentiate between malicious and benign UAV network packets
Ren et al. [4] Algorithm security is crucial in the quickly developing fields of deep learning (DL) and artificial intelligence (AI). Much attention has been drawn to the susceptibility of DL algorithms to adversarial samples, which may fool human observers while inducing misbehaviour in models. Adversarial attacks have generated attention in the machine learning and security fields as they have shown to be feasible in real-world situations. The theoretical underpinnings, algorithms and practical uses of adversarial attack strategies are presented in this work. It also includes research initiatives in the sphere of defensive tactics. The paper’s conclusion highlights unresolved issues to promote more study in this important field
Emimi et al. [5] In healthcare, drones deliver critical medical supplies to remote areas during emergencies. In the armed-services, they enhance security and surveillance efforts. However, drones also pose risks like injuries and property damage, especially when mishandled or experiencing component failures. Extremists can potentially hijack drones for their purposes. This paper comprehensively analyses agricultural, medical and armed-services drones, examining their strengths, weaknesses and opportunities
Lu et al. [6] DNNs are often employed in UAV visual navigation and recognition; yet, they are susceptible to real-time picture perturbations and adversarial camouflage. This research proposes two ensemble techniques for proactive and reactive adversarial defence on UAVs utilising CNN and transformer models. Using adversarial transferability, the proactive method mixes output probabilities from basic DNN models. The reactive method combines average output confidence scores and hidden features with adversarial detectors’ scoring systems. Their usefulness in improving the resilience of recognition tasks is demonstrated by experiments conducted on optical and synthetic aperture radar (SAR) datasets. Additionally, AREP-RSIs, a framework for assessing adversarial resilience in remote sensing models, is introduced in this study
Shankar et al. [7] One revolutionary technique in the Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) is health monitoring. Through the connection of sensors and actuators, data may be captured, monitored, stored and displayed through mobile or online apps. Vital sign monitoring, or IoMT, assists healthcare providers in determining a patient’s state of health and suggesting appropriate treatment. This research presents a novel UAV-integrated health monitoring system that enables clinicians to view and analyse patient data remotely. This IoMT-based UAV system lowers readmissions and hospital stays, however processing large amounts of data presents processing issues. The capability to capture and save video for doctor surveillance together with several parameter measures is a special feature
Elmokadem and Savkin [8] Unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) have rapidly evolved, becoming valuable for various armed-services and public applications, reaching remote areas, saving time and enhancing safety. The focus of UAV development is shifting toward full autonomy, reducing human intervention. This paper surveys recent advancements in UAVs, emphasising safe autonomous navigation, a critical aspect of the system. It delves into advanced methods for 3D avoidance manoeuvres and safe trajectory planning, addressing research challenges in UAV development
Aldaej et al. [9] The development of drones has created new opportunities in many industries, especially for tiny drones. Through the Internet of Things (IoT), they make navigational services possible. However, due to design problems, drone networks provide privacy and security issues, requiring a secure network. This paper highlights the importance of security while examining current privacy and security issues in drone networks (NoD). For data categorization, a hybrid machine learning method that combines random forest and logistic regression is employed. The suggested strategy improves NoD cybersecurity by using cutting-edge AI-inspired techniques, guaranteeing protection. Improved performance is demonstrated via validation using a difficult dataset in terms of statistical metrics and efficiency
Meshram and Reddy [10] The site must be inspected by site owners, architects, structural designers, waterproofing companies, and others for quality control, construction progress and material unloading, among other reasons. Each of these parties needs to visit the site many times in order to do this task, which raises the site’s cost. In addition, a lot of people visit the site, which means that construction and management issues arise frequently. This study suggests a ground-breaking drone-based site monitoring and management system as a workaround. In order to find disparities in material unloading, construction quality, site progress, labour activities and other areas, machine learning techniques are employed in this study. The underlying method is flexible and may be used for many types of events for better upkeep of the site quality. Based on statistical analysis conducted at five sites of varying sizes, the drone-based machine learning model may reduce construction costs by over 10% and increase the quality of construction by 15%, therefore increasing the overall efficiency of building sites and development
Medaiyese et al. [11] Concerns about security and privacy now have a new angle thanks to drones. Few, if any, strict restrictions regulate who is allowed to purchase or own a drone. This means that anyone may use these planes to sneak into areas that are private or restricted. Using a drone detection and identification (DDI) system is one way to find and identify drones in a given region. DDI systems can utilise a range of sensing modalities, including radio frequency (RF) signals, video, sounds and thermal imaging, to identify an invading drone. In this research, we saw an RF-based machine learning DDI system based on low band RF data from the drone-to-flight controller link. We develop three machine learning models that detect and identify drone presence, kind and operation mode using the XGBoost algorithm. These three XGBoost models were analysed using 10-fold cross validation, and the average accuracy that we were able to obtain was 99.96%, 90.73%, and 70.09%, respectively
Sachdeva et al. [12] Unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) are susceptible to various cyberattacks and vulnerabilities. Additionally, they can be seized and utilised by illicit organisations, leading to cybercrime. These vulnerabilities can be used by adversaries to cause data loss, property damage and even fatalities. While keeping an eye on UAV activities can assist in identifying attacks like GPS spoofing, jamming, false information dissemination and blackhole and grey hole development, it may not allay privacy concerns. This paper explains how UAVs may communicate securely using blockchain technology. Our approach involves building a trustworthy and secure UAV ad hoc network as well as smart contracts. This network will be secure from malevolent attacks and resilient to a wide range of network hazards

Research examines how UAV technology interacts with the Internet of Things (IoT) and how this may affect smart cities in the future. The widespread use of drones in these metropolitan environments presents significant risks to public safety, privacy and cybersecurity. The increased use of drones has spurred development on secure data gathering and storage systems such as SecureIoD, which uses blockchain-based storage methods, secure key establishment and authentication to protect IoD data. Although difficulties with managing massive data quantities still exist, the integration of UAVs into the Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) has produced creative health monitoring systems that enable medical practitioners to access and analyse data remotely.

More autonomy and less human involvement are the goals of UAV technological advancements, with a focus on safe navigation methods. Strong cybersecurity standards are required in light of privacy and security vulnerabilities in drone networks. The use of ML techniques, like decision tree and logistic regression, has shown improved cybersecurity protocols for drone networks. Furthermore, a number of studies highlight how artificial intelligence (AI) and ML are revolutionising a number of drone-related fields, including intrusion detection systems, site monitoring and communication security.

Blockchain technologies, 5G networks and AI all work together to create potentially intelligent and safe drone communication systems as shown in Figure 4.2. Additionally, with an emphasis on security in smart cities, AI-driven 5G apps provide cutting-edge capabilities in virtual emotion systems. Blockchain-based solutions show promise in protecting networks from criminal attacks and network threats while also safeguarding communication in UAV systems. As this is going on, intelligent intrusion detection systems are being developed to combat common threats to UAVs. These systems use techniques such as PCA and single-class classifiers to identify and neutralise attacks like jamming and spoofing, protecting UAVs even in situations where communication is disrupted.

4.3 Comparative analysis based on focus, methodology and results of referenced papers

4.3.1 Machine learning-based approaches

‘Machine-Learning-Enabled Intrusion Detection System for Cellular Connected UAV Networks’ security model creation for 5G and satellite-based UAV networks, employing ML for packet distinction and attack identification. The research examines the consequences of combining UAVs, 5G networks and satellite technologies, with an emphasis on the recent boom in UAV deployment across several sectors. It tackles the growing security issues with UAV nodes, which are vulnerable to assaults because to inherent weaknesses and increasing data volumes. The study offers unique UAVs using the 5G network security model that utilise ML approaches to identify and categorise several forms of modern attacks on UAV networks. Comparative evaluations are carried out to analyse the performance and accuracy of ML algorithms, with a focus on the decision tree algorithm’s usefulness in identifying different UAV network threats [3].

‘Adversarial Attacks and Defences in Deep Learning’ examines vulnerabilities of DL algorithms to adversarial attacks in UAVs, discussing defence techniques.

This study focuses on the susceptibility of DL algorithms used in UAVs to adversarial assaults. It dives into the theoretical underpinnings, methods and real-world applications of adversarial attack strategies on DL algorithms.

The paper provides a thorough review of protection mechanisms in response to adversarial attacks, encompassing the domains of ML and security research [4].

‘Adversarial Robustness Enhancement of UAV-Oriented Automatic Image Recognition Based on Deep Ensemble Models’ uses CNN and transformer models, a paper that describes research conducted on optical and synthetic aperture radar (SAR) datasets.

Using transformer models and convolutional neural networks (CNNs), the study suggests and assesses two ensemble methods for proactive and reactive adversarial defensive mechanisms in UAVs. Whereas the reactive technique combines adversarial detector scoring functions with average output confidence ratings and hidden features, the proactive method combines adversarial detector scoring functions with average output confidence scores and hidden features.

Optical and SAR datasets are used in experiments to test the defensive mechanisms’ ability to improve recognition task performance, especially in hostile environments [6].

‘Smart IoMT Framework for Supporting UAV Systems with AI’ integrates health monitoring with UAVs but lacking specific methodology details and introduces a novel Health Monitoring System connected with UAVs, allowing medical practitioners to view and analyse data remotely. This UAV system based on IoMT intends to minimise hospital stays and readmissions [8].

‘Smart cybersecurity framework for IoT-empowered drones: machine learning perspective’ shows the technique focuses on the privacy and security vulnerabilities that exist in drone networks (NoD) and emphasises the importance of developing a secure network architecture. The paper dives into current privacy and security problems raised by drone networks, attributing them to network design defects. To address these concerns, the study utilises a hybrid ML approach for successful data categorization that combines logistic regression and random forest algorithms. The technique incorporates sophisticated AI-inspired approaches, which contribute to the enhancement of cybersecurity measures in NoD networks. Data collecting from difficult datasets related to NoD privacy and security problems is part of the study process. The validation method results in an evaluation of the suggested approach’s efficiency and statistical performance metrics, demonstrating improvements in NoD network cybersecurity and overall protection [10].

‘Machine Learning Applications in Internet-of-Drones: Systematic Review, Recent Deployments, and Open Issues’ discusses the broad utility of drones/UAVs and their adaptability in various fields. This research looks into the application of ML in the context of the IoD and UAVs. It examines IoD/UAV ideas, ML applications, current breakthroughs, existing approaches and future research prospects in depth. CNNs are frequently used in IoD-UAV applications, with resource and mobility management gaining the greatest focus, according to the findings. Python is the most popular programming language and publishing activity peaked in 2021. The study emphasises the different potential uses for drones across several industries, emphasising their versatility and practicality in a variety of circumstances. This shows the wide range of applications for drones [13].

‘Development of a Machine Learning-Based Drone System for Management of Construction Sites’ proposes a drone-based site monitoring system to enhance site quality and reduce construction costs. ML algorithms are used to process site monitoring data collected by drones. To detect abnormalities in material unloading, construction quality and labour activities, these systems use anomaly detection and classification techniques. The solution employs a scalable event-based architecture for site quality management on an ongoing basis. It is anticipated that the use of ML algorithms for site monitoring would result in a significant 10% reduction in building costs and a 15% improvement in construction quality. This enhancement shows how effective drone site management can be [10].

‘Machine Learning Framework for RF-Based Drone Detection and Identification System,’ focuses on ML-based drone detection and identification solutions to solve privacy and security concerns. Using low band radio frequency data from drone-to-flight controller communications, it applies ML models for drone identification and detection through RF signal analysis. Ten thorough cross-validations are performed on the models to ensure accuracy.

The use of ML algorithms for site monitoring is predicted to significantly cut construction costs by more than 10% while improving building quality by 15%. This advancement demonstrates the effectiveness of deploying drones for site management [11].

‘Deep Learning Driven Security in Digital Twins of Drone Network’ details a security study using improved LSTM for drone attack prediction, protecting data privacy using differential privacy and testing model performance using Tennessee Eastman process. Cyberphysical system (CPS) data is evaluated using an advanced LSTM network, with a focus on predictive modelling for drone control signal data before an attack. Through the utilisation of the Tennessee Eastman process as a simulation platform to assess the prediction model, the solution employs differential privacy techniques to protect data privacy. An augmented LSTM network paired with differential privacy approaches is expected to increase prediction accuracy and robustness in drone attack prediction. This result indicates improved security in drone systems [14].

‘Machine Learning-Based Delay-Aware UAV Detection and Operation Mode Identification over Encrypted Wi-Fi Traffic’ describes methods for UAV detection and operating mode identification utilising encrypted Wi-Fi communication characteristics.

Wi-Fi communication metrics like packet size and interarrival time are employed for UAV identification and detection. The method that the system uses for feature selection and prediction performance improvement is called reweighted l1-norm regularisation.

Wi-Fi data may be used to recognise different consumer UAVs with high accuracy (85.7–95.2%) and fast recognition times (0.15–0.35 seconds). This demonstrates the capacity to promptly and precisely detect UAVs [15].

‘Securing Internet of Drones Networks Using AI-Envisioned Smart-Contract-Based Blockchain’ presents a security architecture for secure communication using blockchain in the IoT/IoDs domain and proposes the use of blockchain technology to provide safe communication between drones in the context of IoT/IoDs. Smart contracts and peer-to-peer cloud servers (P2PCS) enable safe and encrypted data exchanges, while blockchain approaches ensure consensus.

The use of blockchain to secure drone communication is expected to guarantee secure and encrypted communication while protecting data privacy among drones [16].

‘Fusion of blockchain and artificial intelligence for secure drone networking underlying 5G communications’ proposes a secure drone communication architecture based on blockchain that makes use of 5G and AI technologies.

This article provides a blockchain-based architecture combined with 5G communication networks and AI algorithms for secure drone communication and enhances communication security and network performance by leveraging the InterPlanetary File System (IPFS) as a platform for data storage. 5G networks and AI algorithms are used to provide a dynamic, adaptive, and secure communication infrastructure for drones. This configuration is considered to be beneficial, especially in healthcare contexts [17].

‘Research Challenges and Security Threats to AI-Driven 5G Virtual Emotion Applications Using Autonomous Vehicles, Drones, and Smart Devices’ discusses AI-based risks in 5G-enabled smart cities and introduces 5G-I-VEmoSYS, a 5G integrated virtual sentiment system powered by AI. AI-based security issues are discussed in 5G-enabled smart cities and offered a 5G integrated virtual sentiment system powered by AI, hinting at AI uses for drone communication monitoring and security. A 5G integrated virtual sentiment system powered by AI for monitoring and safeguarding drone communication in smart city environments are being developed. The goal is to provide dependable and secure communication networks [18].

‘Artificial intelligence for intrusion detection systems in Unmanned Aerial Vehicles’ introduces a system to detect instrusions (IDS) for UAVs using PCA and single-class classifiers, tackling GPS spoofing and jamming. In an IDS developed to detect GPS spoofing and jamming threats against UAVs, PCA and single-class classifiers are used. This system operates within the UAV’s resource-constrained agent devices and sims at high accuracy rates of over 90% in detecting and perhaps neutralising GPS spoofing and jamming assaults against UAVs [19].

4.3.2 Other approaches for security in drones

‘Drones for Smart Cities: Issues in Cybersecurity, Privacy, and Public Safety’ highlights public safety as key considerations when identifying drone-related issues in future smart cities.

The study report took a progressive stance, projecting how drones will be employed in networked smart cities in the future. It outlines a wide range of possible duties, such as broadband wireless access, delivery services, monitoring and security systems. The report also highlights the technological and social challenges associated with the widespread deployment of drones in future smart cities, notably with regard to public safety, cybersecurity and privacy.

With an emphasis on how drone integration may affect cybersecurity, the survey article creates a comprehensive approach by reviewing and synthesising recent studies, publications and literature on the topic of drone integration into smart cities in the future [2].

‘The Current Opportunities and Challenges in Drone Technology’ highlights UAV applications in agriculture, healthcare and armed-services sectors. The study of the many applications of drone technology in agriculture, healthcare and armed-services areas. It examines drones’ roles and contributions in various industries in depth, highlighting their advantages, potential hazards and potential misuses. The study examines the advantages, disadvantages, possibilities and potential risks provided by drones in agriculture, healthcare and armed-services settings [5].

‘SecureIoD: A secure data collection and storage mechanism for internet of drones’ explains the creation and testing of SecureIoD, an IoD data collection system that is secure.

Given the limits of drones and the open wireless medium, this study emphasizes the need for data security and privacy in the IoD scenario. SecureIoD, a secure IoD data gathering and storage solution, is introduced in the study. It consists of mutual authentication between drones and zone service providers (ZSPs), secure key creation and blockchain-based data storage.

The miner ZSP is selected via a hybrid proof-of-work and proof-of-stake consensus mechanism. Practical tests and simulations demonstrate that SecureIoD is more secure and effective than previous systems, and security studies support its resistance to attacks [20].

‘Towards fully autonomous UAVs: A survey’ highlighting recent advancements in UAVs and safe autonomous navigation methods. The article starts by underlining the progress of UAVs across many applications in the armed-services and public sectors, emphasising its worth in remote access, time savings and safety advancements. The research focuses on current UAV advancements, particularly in terms of reaching full autonomy and eliminating human interaction. The study is to assess and evaluate improvements in UAV technology, with a focus on safe autonomous navigation. The process includes a thorough assessment of current literature, technical reports and research papers to accomplish this. Advanced approaches for 3D avoidance manoeuvres and trajectory planning, addressing unique research issues related with UAV development, particularly in the context of safe autonomous navigation [9].

‘Privacy and Security Improvement in UAV Network using Blockchain’ addresses UAV vulnerabilities and proposes using blockchain to secure UAV communication. Proposes blockchain technology for protecting UAV communication, with an emphasis on smart contracts and the establishment of a secure UAV ad hoc network that is resistant to different network attacks and intrusions. A blockchain-based secure UAV ad hoc network is envisioned to withstand different network attacks and provide a safe and dependable communication environment for UAVs [19].

‘Intrusion detection systems in Unmanned Aerial Vehicles’ introduces an intrusion detection system for UAVs using PCA and single-class classifiers, tackling GPS spoofing and jamming. In an intrusion detection system developed to discover GPS spoofing and jamming threats against UAVs, PCA and single-class classifiers are used. This system operates within the UAV’s resource-constrained agent devices and aims at high accuracy rates of over 90% in detecting and perhaps neutralising GPS spoofing and jamming assaults against UAVs [20].

4.4 Conclusion

Throughout our exploration of research in the field of UAVs and drone technology, we have uncovered critical advancements and challenges that shape this innovative domain. UAVs have become indispensable in both military and civilian applications, offering significant improvements in remote accessibility, efficiency and safety. The push toward fully autonomous UAVs represents a major leap forward, aiming to reduce human intervention while prioritizing safe, autonomous navigation. This progress has been examined through techniques for 3D obstacle avoidance and trajectory planning, addressing key challenges in the field. Simultaneously, the rapid expansion of smaller drones has opened new possibilities across various sectors, especially through the integration of the Internet of Things (IoT) for navigation services. However, this growth brings with it serious privacy and security risks within drone networks (NoD), stemming from inherent design vulnerabilities. Addressing these concerns, we explore a novel hybrid ML approach that combines supervised ML techniques, such as decision-based methods and logistic regression, to enhance NoD cybersecurity. This integrated, AI-driven solution shows promise in strengthening the security of drone networks.
