Chapter 7Document classification engine to segregate multilingual PDF documents

Apeksha Aggarwal and Pawan Kumar

DOI: 10.1201/9781003569619-7

7.1 Introduction

The expedited digitalization of data caused increasing interest toward the paperless electronic documents; automating the process of document classification is becoming a necessity for categorizing the documents properly and knowledge discovery. Deep learning algorithms are used widely for the classification of the electronic documents such as E-mails, blogs, digital libraries, and online news to get some meaningful knowledge out of them. The large and increasing volume of the electronic documents creates the need for intelligent computational techniques to classify and organize the documents to their respective classes automatically. Most of the recent work has been done on the automatic classification of the same language documents. The present work focuses on the extraction of multi-lingual text from the portable document format (PDF) documents.

Classifying such documents from a collection of documents of different languages is an error-prone, tedious, and time-consuming process. The document classification engine can assist humans in the separation of documents of different languages into different folders. This prevents the document from getting lost if they are not properly labeled and accelerates the process of usage of these documents for proper application. The proposed classification engine can also ingest and classify the PDF documents containing the data in the form of the images. Such document classifier can find applications in various fields like text mining, information extraction systems, etc. The information extraction systems, which can work only on the documents of a specific language require these documents to be segregated before they can be applied for the extraction of the information. Therefore, the document classification engine serves as a prerequisite to classify documents to leverage extraction of information from a variety of PDF documents.

The classification of the documents [1] aids in organization of the documents required for annotation, curation, analysis, and retrieval. A variety of methods have been studied by the researchers for the classification of documents. In general, the work focused on the information and document retrieval mainly utilizes the core technologies behind search engine such as dictionaries and indexing [2]. Considerable work has been performed using these fundamental technologies and improvements have been performed through query reformulation and feedback [3, 4].

Recent work on documents classification involved the use of support vector machines (SVM) [5–7]. SVM algorithm rely on the use of kernel functions to provide separating hyperplanes. Various string kernels are used such as mismatch kernel [8] and the spectrum kernel [9] are used for information retrieval and document classification along with RNA and DNA sequence data.

For example, decision trees [3] and naïve Bayes [10,11] have also been used in the past. Additional methods such as topic modeling, deep learning, etc. have also been explored which can provide better accuracy [12]. Deep learning [13, 14] is also used extensively these days for varied other applications as well. The basic neural network architecture is made up of an interconnected network of nonlinear nodes which are organized in the form of layers. All these layers are organized sequentially in a neural network. However, the task of automatic document classification is becoming more and more challenging with time due to the rapid growth in the size of the corpus in the digitalized era. The size increase has occurred in almost all areas [15].

The chapter organization is given further as: Section 7.2 describes the algorithms used for comparative analysis. Section 7.3 explains in detail about the proposed architecture and methodology. Section 7.4 describes the experiments performed and evaluation metrices as well as the results in details. Section 7.5 concludes the work.

7.2 Techniques used

7.2.1 Naïve Bayes

Naïve Bayes (NB) algorithm is a probabilistic classification algorithm based on Bayes’ theorem. The assumption of Naïve Bayes includes independence of features, where features do not interact with each other while predicting the classification results [16]. The NB classifier is a supervised learning algorithm and can be efficiently trained using small amount of data. Since the variables or the features are assumed to be independent, thus only the calculation of the variances of each class variable is required instead of calculating the values of the entire covariance matrix. Naïve Bayes algorithm is remarkable in terms of performance because it can train on limited data as well [17–21]. Equation (7.1) describes the Bayes Rule below.

$P\left( {c_{i}~\text{|}~D} \right) = \frac{P\left( c_{i} \right)P\text{(}D~\text{|}~c_{i})}{P\left( D \right)}$(7.1)

where $P\left( C_{i} \right) = P(C = c_{i}) = \frac{N_{i}}{N}$

where P(Ci) is the ratio of number of training samples of class Ci in dataset (i.e. Ni) over total number of samples in the dataset (i.e. N)

Although Naïve Bayes is based on the unrealistic assumption of independence between different features but still it delivers reasonable performance for many classification tasks. It is simple and elementary to implement. Therefore, it has been used by the researchers in different tasks [22, 23]. In [24], the Naïve Bayes classifier performed exceptionally well where the probabilities computed by the model were not important. It is used for different classification applications like spam filtering [25] in emails, spam categorization, and categorization of web content.

7.2.2 Long short-term memory model

Long short-term memory (LSTM) was proposed in [26] which has proven to be extremely powerful in the past for learning sequences over standard recurrent neural networks (RNNs). RNNs are basically neural networks unfolded over multiple time steps and thus can process sequential data such as time series and sentences. With each time step having hidden state, processing inputs from the previous hidden state and current inputs. The disadvantages of RNNs are vanishing and exploding gradients. The transition functions of the LSTM [26] are defined as per Equation (7.2)–(7.7): it = σ(Wi.[ht − 1,xt]+bi)(7.2)

ft = σ(Wf.[ht − 1,xt]+bf)(7.3) ot = σ(Wo.[ht − 1,xt]+bo)(7.4)
ct = ft⊙ ct − 1 + it ⊙ qt
(7.5) ht = ot ⊙ tanh(ct)(7.6)

In the above equations, ⊙ signifies the element wise multiplication operation. The notations are: current time step xt, old hidden state ht-1, the input gate it, the forget gate ft, and the output gate ot. Weights are denoted by Wo, Wq, and Wi which are trainable parameters with respect to different gates. Previous and current memory cell are denoted by ct-1 and ct, respectively The current hidden state ht is modified using ct and is collectively decided by these gates. Figure 7.1 depicts the LSTM cell diagram.

7.2.3 Bidirectional long short-term memory (Bi-LSTM) model

Bidirectional LSTM (Bi-LSTM) models are helpful in extracting more contextual information [27]. The data processing is performed in the backward and forward direction simultaneously [28] and the LSTM outputs are merged using the sum statistic [29].

Bi-LSTM models serve as state-of-the-art models for classifying the sequential data into multiple classes and have been used extensively for speech recognition [30, 31, 32, 33, 34]. The text in the pages of the PDF documents is also a sequence of words and multiclass criteria also exist as the documents are to be classified into the respective language class. Therefore, Bi-LSTM model is applied for the classification task in this paper.

7.3 Methodology

7.3.1 Dataset description

Dataset comprises pdf files from three languages, viz. English, Slovakian, and Czech language. Data cleaning and preprocessing is performed over the sentences. Count Vectorizer is used to encode set of sequences. Dataset is split as: 80% of data is used for training and remaining 20% is used in testing. Furthermore, cross validation is used to avoid the overfitting to a single sample set. Data are trained for 100 epochs which is selected by using grid search methodology. Table 7.1 shows the dataset details.

Table 7.1 Dataset samples⏎
Class Number of samples
English 5000
Slovakian 5000
Czech 5000

7.3.2 Feature extraction

Before starting with the analysis, firstly the extraction of the text from the PDF document file is performed. Tesseract OCR system is used to extract the content from a document page if the page is present in the form of a raster image [15]. After extracting, the text passes through document classification engine, explained in detail in Section 7.3.3.

7.3.3 Document classification engine

The document classification engine can assist humans in the separation of documents of different languages into different folders. This prevents the documents from getting lost if they are not properly labeled and accelerates the process of usage of these documents for proper application. The proposed classification engine can also ingest and classify the PDF documents containing the data in the form of images. Document classification engine serves as a prerequisite to classify documents to leverage extraction of information from a variety of PDF documents.

Figure 7.2 depicts methodological steps for carrying out classification. Initially, preprocessing is done, which includes removal of stop words using NLTK library in Python. Further missing values, interpolation, and stemming are performed in the dataset. Later on feature selection is performed.

Since most of the text was obtained from the image format pages of the PDF by applying the OCR on the raster scanned documents with noise, many out-of-vocabulary words were found in the extracted data. Such problem can be resolved either by running a named entity recognition (NER) [35] system or by the use of stemming and multiple regular expressions [36]. In this work, we have used stemming as a preprocessing step.

7.4 Experiments and results

7.4.1 Experimental details

For carrying out experiments, we have utilized Spyder 3.2.8 notebook with Python version 3.6.5. The experiments are executed on Windows 11, 64-bit OS system having 16GB RAM, with Intel(R) i5 CPU @ 2.40 GHz. Python Tensorflow and Keras API are used primarily to implement various algorithms in this work.

7.4.2 Baseline models

7.4.2.1 DNN: deep neural network

Architectural details of DNN are defined in Figure 7.3.

7.4.2.2 Lstm

Relu activation is used in all the layers except for last layer. Binary cross entropy loss is used as a loss function and for optimization, Adam is used for hyperparameter tuning across multiple epochs.

7.4.2.3 BiLSTM

The proposed architecture composed of embedding layer, spatial dropout layer, and a single Bi-LSTM Layer. We have used word embeddings of 128 dimensions as input layer for generating features of sparse input documents. To avoid overfitting, we have used spatial dropout of 1-dimensions with 40% dropping rate. We have further implemented Bi-LSTM layer along with further dropout rate of 20%. The learning parameters utilize grid search technique for optimization. Bi-LSTM layer utilizes 196 neurons as input. This choice of 196 neurons is selected after performing a grid search by selecting neuron range from 32 to 256 neuron sizes, and selecting the one that is giving good performance without overfitting. The final output layer utilizes Softmax function classifying multiple languages based on probabilities. Categorical cross entropy is used as a loss function. Adam is used for hyperparameter tuning as it has given good performance over other hyperparameter tuning algorithms. The output dense layer consists of three neurons for each of the classes. Figure 7.4 shows the architecture of the same.

7.4.3 Results

For evaluation of models, F-score and accuracy metrics are used in this work. Table 7.2 shows results for Bi-LSTM architecture.

Note: Bold value specifies the outperformance of the proposed approach over existing state-of-the-arts.

7.5 Conclusion

Multilingual PDF document classification is an important problem that needs to be addressed owing to the ever increasing size of the electronic documents. This paper uses and compares different algorithms for the classification of multilingual documents. The Bi-LSTM algorithm produced accuracies consistently higher than other conventional algorithms like DNN and LSTM. The advantage of using Bi-LSTM is its ability to learn context in both the directions, which makes it more accurate than LSTM. Also Bi-LSTM requires very less computation requirements, hence it is used in this work. The proposed model has shown the best accuracy with an accuracy of 98.7%. In future, we can comparatively analyze results over much newer models keeping in mind low computing requirements.

Chapter 8FNDetectorFake news detection using combinations of various features

Yun Hwa Wong (Jenny), Amanpreet Kaur, and Sangeeta Lal

DOI: 10.1201/9781003569619-8

8.1 Introduction

In the explosion era of technology and the modern world, fake news is permeating our daily lives at an alarming rate. The Cambridge dictionary has defined “fake news” as “false stories that appear to be news, spread on the internet or using other media, usually created to influence political views or as a joke” [1]. According to the world’s leading public service broadcaster, British Broadcasting Corporation (BBC), the history of fake news started 2000 years ago during a civil war in the Roman Republic. Octavian wanted to win the war, and he disseminated the “fake news” against Mark Anthony by printing the slogan on the coins and distributing it to the public. In the 19th century, after the technique of print pressed was invented. One of the newspapers in 1835, articles were published about fantastic creatures such as flying batmen and unicorns living on the moon [2].

Before the concept of online social media came, most of the people get news via reading newspapers or listening to the broadcast channel on the radio. With the characteristic of connecting people and sharing content easily, the rate of disseminating news has increased drastically via online social media.

A study conducted by [3] on the Twitter dataset from the year 2006 to 2017 shows that between 1000 and 100000 people were affected by the top 1% of fake news while the truth was seldom spread to more than 1000 people. The study has shown how fast fake news can be disseminated on online social media.

Fake news is prevalent in many domains, especially in the domain of politics. During 2016 US presidential nomination process, the fake news about Donald Trump was greatly favored and has been shared extensively on social media. On Facebook, there were 115 fake news stories supporting Trump and 41 supporting Clinton [4]. The possible impact of fake news disseminated on public forum has drawn up particular attention from different researchers and the public. Fake news, however, is not just about political issues. In recent years, fake news can be disseminated widely when a disaster or pandemic happens. The best example is the SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19) virus breakout that happened in the year 2019. There are tons of fake news or false information related to information on how to get recover from the disease such as eating hot pepper can help to prevent or cure COVID-19, and drinking alcohol can protect the public against COVID-19 [5]. For example, fake news written as a Facebook post by a user regarding steaming can kill the Coronavirus and has been shared more than 33,000 times on Facebook [6]. Researchers have put great deal of time and effort to develop different algorithms and models to detect fake news. A study of categorizing different approaches used by various researchers has been done by de Beer and Matthee [7]. According to de Beer and Matthee [7], the approaches used by existing researchers can be categorized into five main categories (refer to Table 8.1). An example of each of the approaches can be found in Table 8.1.

Table 8.1 Example or descriptions of each approach to identify fake news⏎
Approaches Examples/Descriptions
Language approach Topic-agnostic approach Machine learning approach Bag of Words, Semantic Analysis, Deep Syntax Using the linguistic features and web mark-up capabilities Deep learning neural network
Knowledge-based approach Expert oriented, computational oriented and crowd sourcing oriented fact checking
Hybrid approach Combination of different models or techniques

Despite the myriad of methods and techniques proposed by researchers over the years to identify fake news, it remains the case that, as of now, no approach has achieved a level of detection with complete accuracy, to the best of our understanding. There are two main reasons:

Data challenge: Researchers can only use a few publicly available data sets, as far as we are aware, to develop and test their fake news detection models. Different data sets are suitable for different tasks. It is challenging to get the correct dataset such as the LIAR dataset introduced by Wang [8] that fulfills the research’s requirements. However, these have limitations in terms of feature set availability and are not suitable for the work proposed in this paper.

Identification of an adequate learning model: The dataset and extracted feature have a significant influence on the learning model’s performance [9]. Hence, it is important to carefully identify the features of a given data set. We have presented the FNDetector model. We used features from four domains: linguistic-based features, sentiment-based features, name entity recognition (NER)-based features, and textual features. We trained two variants of the FNDetector model, one using the Random Forest (RF) classifier and another using the long-term short memory (LSTM) classifier. We assessed the efficiency of the FNDetector model on Horne2017FakeNews Dataset. Experimental results show that FNDetector outperforms the baseline classifier significantly. The FNDetector model using the RF classifier outperforms the baseline classifier by 9.3% (F-measure) and 9.8% (accuracy) and FNDetector using the LSTM classifier outperformed the baseline classifier by 40.2% (F-measure) and 17.7% (accuracy). Following are the main research questions that we answered using this study.

  • RQ 1: How well the baseline classifier perform?
  • RQ 2: How well does the suggested model, FNDetector, perform in comparison to the baseline classifier?
  • RQ 3: How important to extract features from both title and contents of the article?
  • RQ 4: Which features are more important in the FNDetector model?
  • RQ 5: What is the consequence on FNDetector model’s efficiency if the parameters of RF and LSTM classifiers are changed?

This is how the chapter is structured. We introduced fake news in Section 8.1. We will provide background information about fake news in Section 8.2. Section 8.3 reveals the particulars of contribution this study makes to the field of original research and will address related work. In Section 8.4, we will give detailed information about the proposed FNDetector model. In Sections 8.5, 8.6, and 8.7, we will provide details related to dataset extraction, experimental setup, and metrics for assessment. Section 8.8 will provide motivation, approach, and the results obtained for each Research Question (RQ). In Section 8.9, conclude and future directions will be provided.

8.2 Background

Various forms of fake news can be circulated in our daily life. It is circulated in different formats such as videos, URLs, articles, etc. However, they are serving the same purpose and motivation which is the creator would like to get attention and unethical benefits from the public. Here, we will discuss different types of fake news that we encounter in our daily life and how the creator gets an advantage from it.

8.2.1 Clickbait

The clickbait can be represented by a link or thumbnail on online social media. It has become one of the dominant forms of medium to spread fake news on social media. The headline of clickbait is always misleading and can involve a curiosity gap from the public, gossip, or sensational aspect to attract the user to click on it [10]. A survey that has been conducted among 53 Standford students found that 96.2% of them came across clickbait stories online at least once each day [11]. The motivation for creating clickbait from the creator is to increase the number of websites visited and advertising avenues.

8.2.2 Propaganda

Propaganda is another form of disseminating fake news. Propaganda is usually used to distribute biased or misleading information that is related to political aspects or personal points of view before the election or protest happens in order to influence people’s opinion. To achieve the greatest impact, propaganda employs strategies of subtle psychological and linguistic [12]. Due to the nature of propaganda, the heinous propaganda news sources have demonstrated their ability to have a significant influence. The power of propaganda and disinformation has been illustrated in recent events such as Brexit. Propaganda propagated on a notorious bus [13] has become a significant representation of the Brexit referendum campaign [14]. The false claim has been believed by half of the British public which has brought a negative impact during and after the referendum.

8.2.3 Satire and parody

The satire and parody is a humorous commentary on a work that calls for an imitation of the work or criticism of the world. The comic effect will be applied to both styles to entertain people. Although these do not usually classify as fake news, however, the information present in satire or parody might provide false information or mislead the public and bring a negative impact on the public’s decisions. Examples of the website of satire and parody include The Onion (www.theonion.com/), The Daily Mash (www.thedailymash.co.uk/), etc.

In general, online social media and websites have become one of the dominant platforms for the public to get information. However, false information from social media or websites is difficult to distinguish from the public. Hence, the researchers have invested so much effort for identifying fake news.

8.3 Literature survey and research contribution

8.3.1 Literature survey

To train a model that supports high accuracy in detecting fake news, the dataset required for training the model has played an important role. It is challenging to find a reliable dataset to train a highly efficient model. Shu et al., performed a study on extracted features from different datasets [15]. This extracted feature is separated into two different categories, i.e., news content and social context features. Linguistic-based and visual-based are feature representations types in news content features category. User-based, post-based, and network-based are categorized under social context features. The study discussed about extracted features from some popular datasets: BuzzFeedNews [16], LIAR [17], BS Detector [18], and CREDBANK [19]. The researchers have concluded that each of the datasets mentioned above has restricted some features from being extracted, for example: Only linguistic-based features can be extracted from the BuzzFeedNews dataset. The dataset is not supporting any existing feature extraction. Hence, to defeat the downside of existing dataset, the researchers have developed a new dataset, i.e., FakeNewsNet, to include all the news information and social context features.

Bhutani et al. [20] used three different datasets: LIAR [17], George McIntire7 [21], and merged dataset (the combination of the dataset from Kaggle [22], PolitiFact [23], and the Emergent dataset). They applied different text preprocessing techniques such as Bi-grams, Tri-grams, CountVectorizer, etc on the datasets. Additional columns such as tf-idf scores, sentiments, and cosine similarity scores have been added to the merged dataset. Therefore, by extracting the sentiment features from the false news detection model, accuracy of fake news identification is improved.

Khan et al. [24] and Iwendi et al. [25] carried out the investigation on the importance of feature extraction. Both of the studies extracted the feature from the text like the count of lowercase characters, average word length, positive sentiment score, etc., before passing them to advanced machine learning algorithms (RF algorithms, deep learning algorithms). The prediction precision of fake news detection before feature extraction by means of a decision tree classifier was 67.81%. However, after applying feature extraction, the result has significantly improved by 10% giving 77.58% prediction accuracy. Ajao et al. [26] included the sentiment element as one of the extracted features. The sentiment analysis used in the study was the emotional ratio score. The authors were using the latent semantic analysis and latent Dirichlet allocation to retrieve the emotional scores. As a result, the model which included emotional ratio gave better results than the existing extracted features that were used on the same dataset. Choudhary and Arora [9] developed three models to experiment on the prediction precision of fake news detection. The final result of the experiment shows Model 3 based on all linguistic features has achieved better accuracy over the other two models (Model 1 based on grammatical, sentimental, and syntax-based features and Model 2 based on readability features) which achieved 84.52% average accuracy.

The above studies have shown how sentiment analysis could improve the average accuracy of detecting fake news. Besides the traditional features such as textual and environment features that can be extracted to train model, Reis et al. [27] has introduced an extra new feature named as domain localization feature to improve model. The domain localization feature can include IP, city, country, latitude, and longitude extracted from the news website URL. By including the additional feature, the fake news detection model developed by the author manage to detect nearly all the fake news in their dataset.

Although the importance of feature extraction has brought up the attention of the researchers, however, the classification approaches used to build the fake news detection model are a force to be reckoned with. Khanam et al. [28] has performed a study on adopting different traditional classification algorithms for fake news. XGboost outperformed with an accuracy greater than 75%. Besides the traditional classification algorithm, the researchers such as [29] applied distinct the deep learning model to the dataset for comparison. As a result, most deep learning models gave superior performance than traditional classification algorithm. Though Naive Bayes is a commonly used method, it has great potential in detecting fake news, nearly matching the efficacy of deep learning models. On top of traditional and deep learning models, Khan et al. [30] has conducted a benchmark research on various machine learning models including the pretrained model such as BERT [31]. Result of pretrained BERT-based model was better than other models.

Furthermore, the researcher also tried to propose the latest technology such as blockchain as a solution [32]. There are a few challenges and limitations in adopting blockchain technology in fake news detection but the author believed that in the near future, detecting fake news would be much easier with the proposed strategy. Moreover, some existing libraries have been built, and the Flair library [33] is one of them. Flair allows the user to combine different kinds of word embedding such as BERT embedding, ELMo transformer embedding, etc [34]. Researcher in [35] has used the Flair library for building model. The study found out that by using the Flair library with a deep learning algorithm, the accuracy of detecting true and false information was up to 99.8%.

The study of distinguishing between fake news, real news, and satire was performed by Horne and Adali [36]. The researcher has used three different datasets including the Buzzfeed election data set, Burfoot and Baldwin data set, and random political news data set that was created by the researcher from different sources. Three main features: psychological, stylistic, and complexity features were extracted from the dataset and run on the SVM model with a linear kernel. Result shows that accuracy of separating fake and real news from body text of the article was 71% on the random political news dataset and 77% on the Buzzfeed election dataset.

Balshetwar et al. [37] worked on detecting fake news and analyzing sentimental behavior present in the text. They used sentiment present in the text to cues of emotional signals. They tested the proposed model on the ISOT and LIAR datasets. The projected approach achieved precision of 99.8%.

Uppada et al. [38] proposed a model that uses for text and images. They used a fine-tuned BERT model for text processing. Their proposed fusion model gave an accuracy of 93%.

8.3.2 Research contributions

We proposed the novel fake news detection model, i.e., FNDetector, using four different types of features (linguistic-based, sentiment-based, NER-based, and textual). We extracted these features from both title and content of the news. We present the results of an in-depth empirical analysis of the FNDetector model on a freely accessible dataset.

Algorithm 1 FNDetector Algorithm

  1. procedure FNDetector(TD)
  2. ALGO = {Arf, ALST M}
  3. : 
  4. for all A ∈ ALGO do
  5. NTTemp = ExtractNewsTitleContent(TD)
  6. NTCorpus = Preprocess(NTTemp)
  7. F = ExtractFeatures(NTCorpus)
  8. : 
  9. if A = Arf then
  10. finalFeature = {FL, FS, FN, FT f}
  11. if A = ALST M then
  12. finalFeature = {FL, FS, FN, FT o}
  13. : 
  14. FNDetector = learnClassifier(finalFeature, A)
  15. return FNDetector
  16. : 
  17. procedure Preprocess(TD)
  18. return Lemmatization with POS(TD)

8.4 Methodology

Figure 8.1 presents the overview diagram, and algorithms 1 and 2 present the detailed steps of the proposed FNDetector model creation. For details about the notations utilized in the Algorithm 1 and Algorithm 2 refer to the Figure 8.2. FNDetector comprises two phases: model building and prediction. In phase 1, we train the model using the training data (step 1 to step 4). In stage 2, we use the FNDetector model to predict fake news (step 5). Following we describe all the steps shown in Figure 8.1 in detail:

8.4.1 Training dataset collection

The experimental dataset is split into a test dataset and a training dataset. We use 80%–20% training and test split. We create models with a training dataset, and we evaluate model’s performance with a test dataset. Prior to training any algorithm, the quality of the dataset must be guaranteed in order to develop a high-performance false news detection model. We load the dataset into a data frame with Pandas library. We shuffle the data before model creation to make sure the dataset is mixing up and not sticking to certain patterns according to the sorted order. We make sure that the dataset does not consist of any null values.

8.4.2 Attribute selection (step 1)

We selected the title and content of news articles for model creation (refer to step 5 in Algorithm 1). We selected both title and content of news articles because we hypothesize that both title and content consist of important information regarding the news and including both of these attributes can be beneficial for separating fake news from real news.

8.4.3 Preprocessing (step 2)

Lemmatization and stemming are popular preprocessing techniques. These techniques were utilized to enhance the performance of document retrieval precision [39]. According to Manning et al. [40], stemming is a rudimentary heuristic process that removes the end of the word and derivational affixes from the word to achieve the goal. Compared to stemming, lemmatization is the process of employing vocabulary and morphological analysis to eliminate a word’s inflectional endings and return the word to its base or dictionary form. Table 8.2 shows the example of stemming and lemmatization.

Table 8.2 Example of stemming and lemmatization⏎
Type Before processed After processed
Stemming Studies, Wander News Studies, Wand New
Lemmatization Studies. Running breathed Study, run breath

In our work, we apply lemmatization to both title and content of the news articles. We did not apply stemming techniques in our dataset as lemmatization achieves improved accuracy and result in comparison to stemming which the result has been shown in the study [39]. However, the default behavior of lemmatization that changes the word’s base to a noun might bring the possibility of reducing the accuracy of our final result outcome. Hence, to minimize the mistake that was brought by default lemmatization, we combine the part-of-speech (POS) tagging and lemmatization in preprocessing the news title and content (refer to step 6 of Algorithm 1). The POS tag, adjective, noun, verb, and adjective are used to identify the specific text in the dataset. So, when the lemmatizer detected a word in the verb from the text, instead of changing it to a noun by default, it will return the base format of the verb. In this work, we are using the lemmatizer from Wordnet [41]. Wordnet is a huge and publicly accessible English lexical database that is used to create organized semantic associations between words. Table 8.3 shows some of the examples from the dataset after lemmatization.

Table 8.3 Example of lemmatization from dataset⏎
Before lemmatization After lemmatization
German prosecutors have closed an investigation into suspected mass phone tapping of German citizens by British and U.S. spies after finding no concrete indication of any criminal activity, they said on Thursday German prosecutors have closed an investigation into suspected mass phone tapping of German citizen by British and U.S. spy after finding no concrete indication of any criminal activity, they said on Thursday

8.4.4 Feature extraction (step 3)

As we mentioned in Section 8.3, feature extraction is crucial to create a high-accuracy model. We extracted features from four different domains. We believe that features extracted from different domains can be beneficial in capturing different aspects of the news, and hence, can aid in improving accuracy (refer to step 7 of Algorithm 1). We extracted 32 features to train the FNDetector model.

8.4.5 Linguistic-based feature

The linguistic-based features are used to categorize the word or phoneme specified in particular pillars such as syntax, semantics, morphology etc. According to Mahyoob et al. [42], words play a significant part in news and the style of writing the news’s contents brings a huge impact on the public. Conroy et al. [43] has mentioned that even though the creator of fake news tries to keep their speech under control, there will still have some verbal “leakage” that is difficult to be controlled such as the pronoun frequencies, use of verbs, conjunction patterns etc. Hence, to detect the “leakage” or “predictive deception indicators” in message content, we need to extract the linguistic-based feature from the dataset. There are two linguistic features we will be focusing on which are grammatical feature and lexical feature:

Grammatical-based feature refers to the structure of the sentences which consists of syntax, morphology, phonology, and semantics. In this project, we extracted the count of noun, verb, adjective, pronoun, and adverb from news headline and body from the dataset. To extract the grammatical feature, we used the concept of POS tagging. POS tagging is also known as grammatical tagging which each of the word sequences in a sentence will be automatic assign or tagged with a grammatical classification which normally contains a verb, adjective, pronoun, noun, etc [44]. Tagging each of the words with different grammar features in a large dataset is tedious and time-consuming. Hence, we took the help of the Natural Language Toolkit (NTLK) to perform the POS tagging.

Lexical-based feature is another type of linguistic-based feature. Lexical is referring the relationship between a word or vocabulary of a language. In our project, we focused on the lexical frequency where the frequencies of the text with correspondences occur in a large corpus such as the number of stopwords like “the”, “is”, etc appear in the title or contents of the article. Besides, we also calculated the count of words in each article.

Table 8.4 provide the details of all the 14 linguistic-based features extracted in this study.

Table 8.4 Details of the Linguistic-based features extracted⏎
Feature’s name Data type Feature domain Description
noun content N Grammatical-based Count of nouns in content
noun title N Grammatical-based Count of nouns in title
pronoun content N Grammatical-based Count of pronouns in content
pronoun title N Grammatical-based Count of pronouns in title
adverb content N Grammatical-based Count of adverbs in content
adverb title N Grammatical-based Count of adverbs in title
verb content N Grammatical-based Count of verbs in content
verb title N Grammatical-based Count of verbs in title
adj content N Grammatical-based Count of adjectives in content
adj title N Grammatical-based Count of adjectives in title
totalWord content N Lexical-based Count of words in content
totalWord title N Lexical-based Count of words in title
totalStopWords content N Lexical-based Count of stopwords in content
totalStopWords title N Lexical-based Count of stopwords in title

Note: N, numeric.

8.4.6 Sentiment-based feature

Sentiment analysis is found to be useful in identifying suspicious accounts from online social media by extracting the element of feelings, attitudes, and opinions [45]. It is also found useful in fake news detection when combined with other extracted features [26]. This happens because fake news often is biased toward one type of emotion. When people are experiencing positive or neutral emotions, they are more inclined to believe a claim to be “true,” yet when they are experiencing negative emotions, they may become more skeptical [46].

Sentiment polarity is one of the important elements in sentiment analysis. It helps to determine how the stated sentiment is oriented. For example, it can help to identify if the text is expressed in negative, neural sentiment, or negative from a statement. In this project, we extracted the polarity scores as one of the features using the sentiment analyzer from the natural language toolkit (NLTK) package [47]. There are four different scores in this package which are positive, negative, neural, and compound score. The polarity score of each score can be ranged from −1 to +1 and the add-up of positive, negative, and neural scores will equal 1. The compound score is total of positive, negative, and neural scores with the range of most extreme negative (−1) to most extreme positive (+1). The more positive the text is, the closer the compound score is to +1. We extract 8 sentiment-based features. Table 8.5 shows the details of the sentiment-based features.

Table 8.5 Details of the sentiment-based features extracted⏎
Feature’sName Data type Description
neg content N Count of negative sentiment in content. Neg
title N Count of negative sentiment in title. Pos
content N Count of positive sentiment in content. Pos
title N Count of positive sentiment in title. Neu
content N Count of neural sentiment in content. Neu
title N Count of neural sentiment in title
compound content N The compound score of sentiment in content
compound title N The compound score of sentiment in title

Note: N, numeric.

8.4.7 Named entity recognition (NER)-based features

NER is a procedure used to identify and classify the designated entities that are presented in a document. According to Shelar et al. [48], the NER tool usually scans through the whole text document and categorizes the entity such as place, organizations, date, major people and etc. It allows for easy content discovery by categorizing the articles in defined hierarchies with the known relevant tags for each text document. With the help of the NER approach, we can identify the relationship between the entities and the truth of news or articles. For example, when an election happens, fake news is tended to have major people’s names as headlines to attract the public. In this work, we are using the trained model “en core web sm” (an English pipeline that includes vocabulary, syntax, and entities with training on written web text documents such as comments, news, and blogs) from the Sparcy library. Table 8.6 shows the example of entities of the NER approach in the trained model. A study from [3] has shown that most of the fake news is related to political news as the false information or political rumors on Twitter spiked during the 2012 and 2016 US presidential elections. This has motivated us to focus on the entities that are more relevant to political news such as organization (ORG), the major person (PERSON), geopolitical (GPE), and money (MONEY) while developing the fake news detection model. We extract eight NER features. Table 8.7 shows the details of all the NER-based features extracted.

Table 8.6 Example of named entities in Sparcy library⏎
Entities Examples
ORGANISATION Apple, Tesla, Amazon
PERSON Donald Trump, Elon Musk
MONEY 1 billion dollar, 2 pound
GPE India, Germany
LOCATION Mount Titlis, Yellow River
DATE 5 JUL, 24 January 2022
TIME two-forty am, 11.09 P.M
Table 8.7 Details of the NER-based features extracted⏎
Feature’s Name Data type Feature Domain Description
person content N NER Count of person in content
content. person title N NER Count of person in title
org content N NER Count of organisation in content. org
content. org title N NER Count of organisation in title. gpe
title. gpe content N NER Count of geopolitical in content. gpe
content. gpe title N NER Count of geopolitical in title. money
money content N NER Count of money in content. money
money title N NER Count of money in title

Note: N, numeric.

8.4.8 Textual features

We have also used textual features in the FNDetector model. We use lemmatized title (with POS) and lemmatized content (with POS) (refer to Table 8.8). However, because textual features cannot be directly fed into the classifiers. We used Term Frequency – Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF) (RF classifier) and tokenizer (LSTM classifier). Following are more details about TF-IDF and Tokenizer. TF-IDF: According to Qaiser and Ali [49], the term frequency (TF) is referring the occurrence of a particular term in a document. To calculate the inverse document frequency of a particular term, we can use the following formula:

$\text{TF} = \frac{\text{Occurrence~of~Words}}{\text{Count~of~words~of~document}}$(8.1)

For example: if a 2000 words document contains the word “library” 5 times. The TF of the word “library” will be 5/2000, which is 0.0025. All the terms play an important role in a document. Inverse document frequency (IDF) aims to assign a weight to a particular term. The IDF algorithm assigns a more weightage to an infrequent term and a lower weightage to a frequent term in the document. To calculate the IDF of a particular term, we can use the following formula:


$$\begin{array}{l} {\text{Inverse~Document~Frequency}\left( \text{IDF} \right)} \\ {\text{~~~} = \log_{e}\frac{\text{Total~number~of~document}}{\text{Number~of~documents~with~term}}} \\ \end{array}$$
(8.2)

For example, if we have 20 documents and the term “library” appears in three documents, the IDF of the term will be loge(20/3) = 1.897. In order to address issues like stopwords that are highly prevalent in documents but have limited significance for text mining, the researchers have suggested merging TD and IDF computations to represent a term’s relative importance to a document within the collection. The Term Frequency – Index Document Frequency (TD-IDF) will assign higher importance to the term that has higher frequency but less occurrence in the total number of documents. To calculate TD-IDF, it is just simply taken the multiplication of TD and IDF. For example, to calculate the term “library” in the collection, we will multiply 0.0025 and 1.897 which is 0.00474.

Table 8.8 Details of the textual features extracted⏎
Feature’s Name Data type Feature Domain Description
title_lem T Textual The lemmatized (with POS) title of articles and news
content_lem T Textual The lemmatized (with POS) content of articles and news

Note: T, textual.

The concept of TD-IDF is playing important role in training the fake news detection model. By using the concept of TD-IDF, we can analyze what are the common terms being used in fake news to attract the public. In our work, the TD-IDF will be applied to the lemmatized title and the content of the articles. To implement the TD-IDF feature in the dataset, the TfidfVectorizer in the scikit-learn package [50] (a machine learning library) is being used.

Tokenizer: The TfidfVectorizer from the scikit-learn package will return the result as a sparse matrix. However, LSTM classifier gives better performance when the sequence is used as input. Hence, for the LSTM classifier, we vectorized heading and body of news article by turning each text into a sequence of integers using Tokenizer [51] (from the TensorFlow package).

8.4.9 FNDetector classifier learning (Step 4)

We combine all the features, i.e., linguistics-based, sentiment-based, NER-based, and textual features. Using this, we obtain a total of 32 features. Using these we train two versions of the FNDetector model, one using the RF classifier (FNDetectorRF) and the other using the LSTM classifier (FNDetectorLSTM). The configuration related details of both the algorithms are shown in Table 8.9. Following are the details of both of the algorithms:

Table 8.9 The configuration details of RF and LSTM classifiers used in this work⏎
Method Setting
RF Classifier n estimators = 100
LSTM epochs = 10

Random Forest (RF) Classifier: Breiman et al. [52] first presented the RF method, an ensemble learning approach for regression trees and classification. A collection of classifiers with tree structures make up the RF classifier, and each tree votes for the class that is most popular.

Compared to other machine learning algorithms, the random forest doesn’t consist of overfitting problems due to the use of the Strong Law of Large Numbers. The core method for the random forest to improve its accuracy was using the bagging method (also known as Bootstrap aggregating [53]. Besides, the out-of-bag method was used to estimate the strength, correlation, and generalization error. Furthermore, based on the strength of each predictor and their correlations, the algorithm offers information about the random forest’s prediction ability.

According to Breiman [52], the random forest provides significant improvement in classification from the concept of expanding the collection of trees and letting them choose the most popular class.

Figure 8.3 describes RF algorithm. Taking into account that the training dataset includes balls of various hues, including pink, green, blue, yellow, and red.

The dataset will be divided into subsets and assigned to one decision tree in the RF algorithm. Each of the decision trees produces one output. For example, decision tree 1 and 2 produces yellow ball as their output, and tree n produces blue ball as their output. Then, the random forest algorithm will collect the majority voting and produce the final prediction. In our example, most of the tree has selected the yellow ball as their prediction output. Hence, the final prediction of RF will be the yellow ball.

Many studies such as [54] have shown that RF is among the machine learning methods with the highest performance, particularly for prediction tasks. The RF algorithm was formed by a tree-based model. The random forest algorithm is being used widely across different areas such as stock price prediction in the financial industry [55]. There are also lot of researchers who had used the random forest as the classifier to train fake news detection model as mentioned in Section 8.3. In our work, we tend to use different extracted features with RF algorithms to experiment with prediction accuracy.

Long-short term memory neural network (LSTM): The pioneer of the LSTM is the recurrent neural network (RNN) that utilizes consecutive or time series data. The RNN is often used in time series forecasting such as forecasting the next twelve-month energetic usage of a household. According to Hochreiter and Schmidhuber [56], the existing RNN causes the vanishing and exploding of gradients when the error signals are flowing backward. To overcome this issue, Hochreiter and Schmidhuber [56] has introduced the concept of LSTM. The LSTM was formed by four core elements which are forget gate, input gate, output gate, and a complex unit called memory cell [57]. Different from other traditional deep neural networks, the memory cell in the LSTM algorithm has the center linear unit with fixed self-connection. It goes along the whole chain and makes the selection on which information needs to be stored with the help of three gates [56]. This process allowed the LSTM network to identify long-term dependencies and memorize multiple time dependencies with different characteristics [58].

In our work, we have created the LSTM neural network to train the model. Two embedding layers are created to store the information of the title and content of the article. Then, two LSTM layers with 128 neurons are created to hold the output of embedding layers. A concatenate layer is created to concatenate the output of the two LSTM layers. Furthermore, a dense layer (a layer of neurons that receive the input from the previous layer) is created to process the other numeric features. Another concatenate layer is created to process outcomes from previous two layers before going into another dense layer. Using the input from former layer, the final output layer produce one result as the end product.

The overall LSTM neural network model for our project can be viewed in Figure 8.4. We used the sigmoid function as an activation method for our final output layer. This helps network to learn which data needs to be forgotten or remembered by holding the value between 0 and 1. The information will be forgotten if the value is 0 and stays if the value is 1. Since our final result has only two outcomes which are true or false news, we used binary cross entropy function for providing better performance for the binary classification model.

To perform the experiment using LSTM and answer the research questions in Section 8.8, the maximum number of most frequent words have been defined to 3000 words and the maximum number of words in each title or contents of the article to 300 words. Then, the title and contents of the article will be tokenized by Tokenizer. Three different inputs with the shape of 300 (for the title of the article), 300 (for the contents of the article), and 30 (the other 30 numeric features) will be created for model training. For all the LSTM classifiers, we have trained them with 10 epochs.

To make sure the training model in all experiments presented in Section 8.8 are consistent, we have defined the basic setting for the random forest and LSTM neural network classifier which is shown in the following table:

8.4.10 Prediction phase (step 5)

In this phase, we applied the FNDetector model to guess the label of news articles available in the test dataset. In order to accomplish this, we extract all 32 characteristics after extracting the news items’ titles and contents from the test dataset. To determine whether or not the news article is phony, we feed these features into the FNDetector model.

8.7 Evaluation metrics

To make sure the classification or prediction model is behaving correctly and achieving the goal, a set of checklists will be applied to it to perform the measurement which is known as evaluation metrics. The performance and efficacy of classifiers can be assessed using evaluation metrics [60]. Several factors need to be considered when choosing the correct evaluation metric such as less complexity and less computational cost, the issue of multiclass problems, informativeness, distinctiveness, and discriminable [61]. To ensure a classification model has high efficiency and high performance, it needs to make sure the predicted outcome is the same as the true outcome. The problem discussed in this paper is a binary classification problem. The evaluation metric applied for binary classification problems is the confusion metric which also belongs to the threshold types of evaluation metrics [61].

8.7.1 Confusion metric

The confusion metric is presented in a tabular format. It contains information about the actual and predicted class that is done by a classification model. There are four types of indicators in the confusion metric which are True Positive (TP), True Negative (TN), False Positive (FP), and False Negative (FN) [61].

The confusion metric provides the visualization to the researchers on the indicators so they can take further action on improving the classification model if necessary. Figure 8.5 shows how a confusion metric of random forest classifier. The x-axis and y-axis signify predicted values and real values, respectively. The darker the color in the box of the confusion metric, the higher the values.

8.7.2 Accuracy

The range of accuracy scores is between 0 and 1. In general, the accuracy score calculated the percentage of exact predictions over the total number of samples that are assessed [61]. The equation is listed below:

$\text{Accuracy} = \frac{\text{TP} + \text{TN}}{\text{TP} + \text{TN} + \text{FP} + \text{FN}}$(8.3)

8.7.3 Precision, Recall, and F-Measure

Accuracy score is not a great evaluation metric to judge the performance of a classification model, as it provides a less distinctive value [61]. The accuracy score might not be accurate when the dataset is imbalanced. To overcome the limitation of the accuracy score, we can calculate the precision score of the classification model. The concept of calculating a precision score is using the sample that is precisely predicted from the entire predicted samples in a positive class [61]. Different from precision, the recall score can also be used to calculate the number of positive classes that are correctly classified out of all positive examples in the dataset. The harmonic mean of precision and recall is the f-measure (F1 score). Joshi [62] has mentioned that although the f-measure is less popular being used as the performance metric; however, it performs better than the accuracy score when come to optimizing the classifier for binary classification problems. The range of f-measure can be between 0 and 1. The formulas for computing these measures are listed below: $\text{Precision}\left( p \right) = \frac{\text{TP}}{\text{TP} + \text{FP}}$(8.4)

$\text{Recall}\left( r \right) = \frac{\text{TP}}{\text{TP} + \text{TN}}$(8.5) $F\text{~measure} = \frac{2\text{*}p\text{*}r}{p + r}$(8.6)

8.7.4 Receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve and area under ROC (AUC)

The receiver operating characteristic (ROC) has been widely used as a performance measurement for evaluating medical diagnostic systems [63]. According to Park et al. [64], the ROC is represented as a graph that consists of two key components which are sensitivity (or true positive rate) and specificity (or false positive rate), and it can differentiate the signal from the noise. In a ROC graph, the sensitivity will be plotted on the y-axis and specificity on the x-axis, respectively. The ROC graph can clearly show the relationship between the false positive rate and the true positive rate. The extension of the ROC curve, area under the ROC curve (AUC) has been proved as a useful metric for summarizing the efficacy of the test [65]. Mandrekar [65] also mentioned that the value of AUC can be ranged from 0 to 1, where 0 indicates the test was perfectly inaccurate and 1 indicates that test is perfectly accurate. In general, when the AUC is greater than 0.5, there is a high chance that the classifier can differentiate the positive class value from the negative class value. However, there are some cases where the AUC value is equal to 0.5, and the ROC curve is presented as the diagonal line on the graph. In this case, the classifier will not have the ability to differentiate between the positive and negative classes.

8.8 Experimental results

Here, we provide the details of experimental results.

RQ1: What is the performance of the baseline classifier?

Motivation: We worked on building a fake news detection model using different types of features. It is significant to recognize how the fake news detection model performs without using any of the external feature extraction. Hence, in this RQ, we built our baseline classifier using the title and the content of news articles.

Approach: We used the textual data, i.e., the lemmatized title and content of the news articles to build the baseline classifiers. We then vectorize the dataset by using TfidfVectorizer before training the RF classifier model. For the LSTM classifier, we tokenize the textual data and insert it into an embedding layer before going to the LSTM layer. For this experiment, we use 100 n estimators for RF and 10 epochs for LSTM.

Results: Figure 8.6 and Table 8.11 show the results of this RQ. We obtained an accuracy value of 60.7% and 78.4% for RF and LSTM classifiers, respectively, and an F-measure of 79.2% and 41.1% for RF and LSTM classifiers, respectively (refer to Table 8.10). These results indicate that the baseline classifier gives very low accuracy and F-measure with respect to fake news detection, and hence, there is a need to improve these models. The baseline classifiers perform poorly for fake news detection tasks with RF and LSTM classifiers giving an accuracy of 60.7% and 78.4%, respectively. The performance of the LSTM classifier is much lower than the RF classifier both in terms of accuracy and F-measure.

Table 8.11 The performance of baseline classifiers⏎
Method Accuracy (%) Precision (%) Recall (%) F-Measure (%)
BaselineRF 78.4 80.8 77.8 79.2
BaselineLSTM 60.7 100 25.9 41.1

RQ 2: What is the performance of the proposed model, i.e., FNDetector, when contrasted to the baseline classifier?

Motivation: In this RQ, we investigated the performance of the proposed FNDetector model. To build this model, we combined different types of features. Additionally, we extracted features from both the title and content of news articles. This RQ will help in providing insights into the effectiveness of the FNDetector model.

Approach: To answer this RQ, we trained the FNDetector model using RF and LSTM classifiers to build two variants of the FNDetector model, i.e., FNDetectorRF and FNDetectorLSTM . We tested the performance of these two models on the test dataset and computed various metrics. We also performed a comparison of these two models with the baseline classifiers.

Result: Table 8.12 and Figure 8.7 show the prediction results of the FNDetectorRF and FNDetectorLSTM models. Table 8.12 shows that FNDetectorRF gives an accuracy of 88.2% and an F-measure of 88.5%, whereas FNDetectorLSTM gives an accuracy of 78.4% and an F-measure of 81.3%. Figures 8.8 and 8.10 show the comparison of the performance of the baseline and FNDetector classifiers. Figures 8.9 and 8.10 show that both FNDetectorRF and FNDetectorLSTM models outperform the baseline classifiers in both accuracy and F-measure. We compute the improvements and present the results in the form of bar graphs (refer to Figures 8.10 and 8.11). We notice an improvement of 9.8% (accuracy) and 9.3% (F-measure) using FNDetectorRF model and an improvement of 17.7% (accuracy) and 40.2% (F-measure) using the FNDetectorLSTM model. These results indicate that feature extraction is an important step in fake news model creation. From the result, we can conclude that feature extraction is useful in fake news model building for both random forest and LSTM classifiers. We obtained an improvement of 9.8% (FNDetectorRF) and 17.7% (FNDetectorLSTM) in terms of accuracy and an improvement of 9.3% (FNDetectorRF) and 40.2% (FNDetectorLSTM) in F-measure.

Table 8.12 The performance of the FNDetector model⏎
Method Accuracy Precision Recall F-Measure
FNDetectorRF 88.2% 85.2% 92.0% 88.5%
FNDetectorLSTM 78.4% 74.0% 88.9% 81.3%

RQ 3: How important to extract features from both the title and contents of the article?

Motivation: Most of the researchers have focused on analyzing the headlines of the news articles for fake news model creation such as [66–68]. Some of the datasets that are used for building a fake news detection model such as the LIAR dataset [69]) do not contain both the title and contents of the news articles in the dataset. The dataset only contains the statement extracted from both the title and contents of the article. We believe that the content of news can provide important information for fake news detection. Thus, in this RQ, we work on analyzing the importance of both titles and contents for detecting fake news. The result of this RQ can help us understand which part of the news is more important in fake news detection. Approach: To answer this RQ, we build three variants of the FNDetector model: FNDetector (title), FNDetector (content) and FNDetector (title+content). FNDetector (title), model is trained using only the title of the news articles. FNDetector (content), is trained using the content of the news articles. FNDetector (title+content) is trained using both title and content of the news articles which is the original FNDetector model is proposed in this work. We train all these variants using both RF and LSTM classifiers, hence, create a total of six variants of the FNDetector model.

Results: Tables 8.13, 8.14, and 8.15 show the results of this RQ using both RF and LSTM classifiers. Figures 8.12 and 8.13 show the comparison of the performance between different variants for FNDetectorRF and FNDetectorLSTM models, respectively. Table 8.13 shows that the accuracy and F-measure using FNDetectorRF (title) model is 70.6% and 72.7%, respectively. The FNDetectorLSTM (title) gives accuracy and F-measure of 74.5% and 76.4%, respectively. We observe from Table 8.16 that the performance of the FNDetectorRF model is improved when we use “content” of news article as compared to when we use “title” for training the model. We observe an improvement of 11.8% (accuracy) and 10.3% (F-measure) when using FNDetectorRF (content) as compared to the FNDetectorRF (title) model. For the LSTM classifier, we observe a reduction in the performance in both accuracy and F-measure when the “content” of news articles is used to train the classifier (refer to Table 8.17).

This is also the original FNDetector model proposed in the work. Figures 8.12 and 8.13 show a comparison of the performances of the three variants for FNDetectorRF and FNDetectorLSTM models, respectively. We notice an improvement in the performances of both FNDetectorRF and FNDetectorLSTM models. Using FNDetectorRF (title + content), we notice an improvement of 17.6% (in comparison to FNDetectorRF (title)) and 5.8% (in comparison to FNDetectorRF (content)) in accuracy. Similarly, using FNDetectorLSTM (title+content), we notice an improvement of 3.93% (in comparison to FNDetectorLSTM (title)) and 19.63% (in comparison to FNDetectorLSTM (content)) in accuracy. The improvements are also shown in Tables 8.16 and 8.17. Results indicate that both the title and content of news articles are important in fake news detection. For both FNDetectorRF and FNDetectorLSTM, we observed a significant improvement in the prediction accuracy when both title and content are used to train the model.

These features belong to different domains and have different types, i.e., linguistic-based features (grammatical and lexical features), sentiment-based features, named entity recognition features, and textual features. The FNDetector model outdid the baseline line classifier significantly in both accuracy and F-measure.   • FNDetectorAFS + FL + FN + FT: FNDetector model trained using all the 32 features extracted.

In the above, A refers to the classifier. A can take value RF or LSTM. Hence, we compute the performances of all the above variants with both RF and LSTM classifiers. RQ 5: What is the effect on the performance of FNDetector model by changing parameters of RF and LSTM classifiers?

Motivation: The parameters of an algorithm play an important role as they can impact the performance of an algorithm. It’s critical to adjust an algorithm’s settings., in order to build a high-accuracy model. In this RQ, we will present what is the effect on the performance of the FNDetector model by changing the parameters of RF and LSTM classifiers. We also compute the time it requires to train the models as well as the time required to make the predictions. We believe that time can be an important factor in choosing the appropriate classifier in resource-constrained applications like mobile devices.

Approach: To answer the RQ, we perform several experiments with the FNDetector model using RF and LSTM variants. We create a total of seven variants of the FNDetector model, three using FNDetcetorRF and four using FNDetectorLSTM. For FNDetectorRF, we tested the performance of the model for three different values of the estimator, i.e., 50, 100, and 150. For FNDetectorLSTM, we tested the performance of the model by changing epochs and batch size. We use 2 different epochs, i.e., 5 and 10 epochs, and 2 different batch sizes, i.e., 128 and 256 respectively. Table 8.20 shows the parameters and values that we use to perform the experiments.

The time required to train the model and generate a prediction was then calculated. We compute the time it takes to build the model using the training dataset for both FNDetectorRF and FNDetectorLSTM classifiers for different parameters (number of estimators (for RF classifier) and epoch & batch size (for LSTM classifier)) as discussed above.

Results: Figures 8.17, 8.18, and 8.19 show the result of FNDetectorRF classifier with 50, 100, and 150 n estimators. From the result, we can observe count of FPs and FNs decreases when the n_estimators increase. Also, the AUC value is increased when the n_estimators are increased. These results show that increasing the number of estimators can aid in enhancing the performance of FNDetectorRF (please refer to Table 8.21). For FNDetectorLSTM classifier, we demonstrated the model accuracy and loss of the model by plotting it in Figures 8.20 and 8.21. The results show that the FNDetectorLSTM classifier attained the best result with epoch 10 and batch size 128. We can observe that the loss is decreasing and the accuracy of the model is increasing consistently as the number of epochs increases. This makes the FNDetectorLSTM model with 10 epochs and 128 batch sizes have the highest accuracy in detecting fake news compared to other parameters.

Table 8.21 shows that for FNDetectorRF classifier the training and prediction time increases as we increase the number of estimators. As we are increasing the number of estimators the RF algorithm needs to create more trees and hence, the time increases. For the FNDetectorLSTM classifier, we observe a similar pattern, i.e., training time increase as we intensify the number of epochs. As we increase, batch size the training time decrease for the FNDetectorLSTM classifier. This happened because with increases in batch size the algorithm can take big chunks of the training dataset and can learn faster. Overall, we observe that the FNDetectorRF classifier took much less time in both training and testing as compared to the FNDetectorLSTM classifier. Experimental results show the parameter tuning is very important as it can have a big influence FNDetector model’s effectiveness. In our experiment, the RF classifier achieved its best performance when the n estimator was set to 150 and the LSTM classifier achieve its best performance when epochs value is 10 and the batch size value is 128. FNDetectorRF took much less training and testing time as compared to the FNDetectorLSTM classifier.

8.9 Concluding remarks and future scope

The amount of news items shared on social networking websites has skyrocketed. Because so much false information circulates on social media, people are less likely to believe news articles published on these channels. It’s challenging to confirm the veracity of news articles on social media. The identification of fake news has become more significant as a way to get around this problem and as a motivator for related research. We provide FNDetector, a fake news detection model, in this paper. FNDetector uses a combination of various feature sets. We train the FNDetector using two classifiers, i.e., RF and LSTM. The FNDetector model outperformed the baseline classifier significantly. FNDetectorRF and FNDetectorLSTM give an accuracy of 88.2% and 78.4%, respectively. Our investigation related to feature importance shows that all the feature sets used in this paper are important and help in increasing the accuracy. Also, ‘noun count’ and ‘total word count’ in both the title and content of news articles are found to be the top four important features. We intend to use ensemble learning to expand the model in the future. The ensemble learning model combines different classifiers to improve training models. The ensemble learning model working has been shown in a few studies such as [69] and [45] to improve the outcomes of the models. Additionally, we plan to use more advanced methods such as BERT to enhance the effectiveness of fake news detection.
